I just thought maybe I should get some help with it, if that’s the right thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Does the pain in your jaw stay just in your jaw or does it spread to other areas?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the jaw, I think… but it’s kind of tied in with my neck and shoulders too. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F45.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I… I’m probably making more of it than I should, but what finally pushed me to come in is that my shoulders and neck just stay clenched all day. It’s like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends, if that makes sense. And by the evening my jaw aches so much that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. I just thought maybe I should get some help with it, if that’s the right thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Does the pain in your jaw stay just in your jaw or does it spread to other areas?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the jaw, I think… but it’s kind of tied in with my neck and shoulders too. They stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for something, and then by evening the jaw pain gets bad enough that even playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable. I don’t know if that counts as spreading exactly, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these clenched muscles and jaw pain all the time, or are there times when they go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say it comes and goes, but… most days it’s kind of just there. My shoulders and neck stay clenched pretty much all day, like I’m bracing for something, and then by evening my jaw is usually aching. Sometimes I can ignore it for a while, I guess, but it doesn’t really fully go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your job or the feeling of being observed is what's causing your muscles to clench and your jaw to ache?\n\nPatient: I think it might be part of it, yes… especially that feeling of being watched or evaluated. I don’t know if it’s the whole cause, and I don’t want to overstate it, but my body feels like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. My shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, and then by the evening my jaw aches badly enough that even playing guitar is hard to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're worried about or afraid of that might be contributing to this feeling of being braced or on edge?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, there are a few things. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but my mind keeps circling around whether I’ll ever get hired again, or whether I’ve somehow ruined my reputation with parents.\n\nAnd then it jumps to retirement, like whether that’s slipping away from me. Even little things can turn into something much bigger in my head… like if I make a small mistake during a story-time activity, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. I know that probably sounds extreme, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries about your job and retirement pop into your head throughout the day, or are they something you can put out of your mind for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I can put them aside for a little while, sometimes… or at least I try to. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, make a list,” or I’ll pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing I can do.\n\nBut honestly, it doesn’t always stay put. One worry sort of hands off to the next one, if that makes sense, and then I look up and I’ve lost half the afternoon thinking about it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to redirect it.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries come into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. I don’t know if that counts as spreading exactly, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these clenched muscles and jaw pain all the time, or are there times when they go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say it comes and goes, but… most days it’s kind of just there. My shoulders and neck stay clenched pretty much all day, like I’m bracing for something, and then by evening my jaw is usually aching. Sometimes I can ignore it for a while, I guess, but it doesn’t really fully go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your job or the feeling of being observed is what's causing your muscles to clench and your jaw to ache?\n\nPatient: I think it might be part of it, yes… especially that feeling of being watched or evaluated. I don’t know if it’s the whole cause, and I don’t want to overstate it, but my body feels like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. My shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, and then by the evening my jaw aches badly enough that even playing guitar is hard to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're worried about or afraid of that might be contributing to this feeling of being braced or on edge?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, there are a few things. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but my mind keeps circling around whether I’ll ever get hired again, or whether I’ve somehow ruined my reputation with parents.\n\nAnd then it jumps to retirement, like whether that’s slipping away from me. Even little things can turn into something much bigger in my head… like if I make a small mistake during a story-time activity, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. I know that probably sounds extreme, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries about your job and retirement pop into your head throughout the day, or are they something you can put out of your mind for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I can put them aside for a little while, sometimes… or at least I try to. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, make a list,” or I’ll pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing I can do.\n\nBut honestly, it doesn’t always stay put. One worry sort of hands off to the next one, if that makes sense, and then I look up and I’ve lost half the afternoon thinking about it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to redirect it.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries come into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly… sometimes enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. So yes, it does seem to get worse when I’m worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever pop into your head when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that does happen. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s how it can go.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension easing when you're not worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I think it does ease some when I’m truly not worrying, yes… though honestly that doesn’t seem to happen as often as I’d like.\n\nMost days my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice it starting like a switch flipping.

What I do notice is… when I’m in that worrying place, my shoulders and neck are already clenched, almost all day sometimes, like I’m bracing myself. One worry sort of hands off to the next one, if that makes sense, and then I look up and I’ve lost half the afternoon thinking about it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to redirect it.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries come into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly… sometimes enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. So yes, it does seem to get worse when I’m worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever pop into your head when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that does happen. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s how it can go.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension easing when you're not worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I think it does ease some when I’m truly not worrying, yes… though honestly that doesn’t seem to happen as often as I’d like.\n\nMost days my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension happening at the exact moment you start worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice it starting like a switch flipping.\n\nWhat I do notice is… when I’m in that worrying place, my shoulders and neck are already clenched, almost all day sometimes, like I’m bracing myself. That’s honestly one of the things that pushed me to come in. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly—badly enough that playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it feels connected. I just can’t always pinpoint the exact second it starts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly… sometimes enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. So yes, it does seem to get worse when I’m worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever pop into your head when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that does happen. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s how it can go.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension easing when you're not worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I think it does ease some when I’m truly not worrying, yes… though honestly that doesn’t seem to happen as often as I’d like.\n\nMost days my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension happening at the exact moment you start worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice it starting like a switch flipping.\n\nWhat I do notice is… when I’m in that worrying place, my shoulders and neck are already clenched, almost all day sometimes, like I’m bracing myself. That’s honestly one of the things that pushed me to come in. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly—badly enough that playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it feels connected. I just can’t always pinpoint the exact second it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension start at the same time as your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, more or less, though I don’t always catch it right when it begins.\n\nWhat I notice is that when the worrying is going on, my shoulders and neck are just clenched—sometimes most of the day—like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw aches enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar.\n\nThat was honestly part of what made me think I should come in. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I can just redirect myself, and sometimes I try to do that in a reasonable way—like telling myself, “Okay, make a list,” or “Just focus on the next practical step.”

I’ll tell myself, “Okay, make a list,” or I’ll pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing I can do.\n\nBut honestly, it doesn’t always stay put. One worry sort of hands off to the next one, if that makes sense, and then I look up and I’ve lost half the afternoon thinking about it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to redirect it.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries come into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly… sometimes enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. So yes, it does seem to get worse when I’m worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever pop into your head when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that does happen. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s how it can go.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension easing when you're not worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I think it does ease some when I’m truly not worrying, yes… though honestly that doesn’t seem to happen as often as I’d like.\n\nMost days my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension happening at the exact moment you start worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice it starting like a switch flipping.\n\nWhat I do notice is… when I’m in that worrying place, my shoulders and neck are already clenched, almost all day sometimes, like I’m bracing myself. That’s honestly one of the things that pushed me to come in. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly—badly enough that playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it feels connected. I just can’t always pinpoint the exact second it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension start at the same time as your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, more or less, though I don’t always catch it right when it begins.\n\nWhat I notice is that when the worrying is going on, my shoulders and neck are just clenched—sometimes most of the day—like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. I just thought maybe I should get some help with it, if that’s the right thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Does the pain in your jaw stay just in your jaw or does it spread to other areas?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the jaw, I think… but it’s kind of tied in with my neck and shoulders too. They stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for something, and then by evening the jaw pain gets bad enough that even playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable. I don’t know if that counts as spreading exactly, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these clenched muscles and jaw pain all the time, or are there times when they go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say it comes and goes, but… most days it’s kind of just there. My shoulders and neck stay clenched pretty much all day, like I’m bracing for something, and then by evening my jaw is usually aching. Sometimes I can ignore it for a while, I guess, but it doesn’t really fully go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your job or the feeling of being observed is what's causing your muscles to clench and your jaw to ache?\n\nPatient: I think it might be part of it, yes… especially that feeling of being watched or evaluated. I don’t know if it’s the whole cause, and I don’t want to overstate it, but my body feels like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. My shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, and then by the evening my jaw aches badly enough that even playing guitar is hard to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're worried about or afraid of that might be contributing to this feeling of being braced or on edge?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, there are a few things. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but my mind keeps circling around whether I’ll ever get hired again, or whether I’ve somehow ruined my reputation with parents.\n\nAnd then it jumps to retirement, like whether that’s slipping away from me. Even little things can turn into something much bigger in my head… like if I make a small mistake during a story-time activity, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. I know that probably sounds extreme, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries about your job and retirement pop into your head throughout the day, or are they something you can put out of your mind for periods of time?\n\nPatient: I can put them aside for a little while, sometimes… or at least I try to. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, make a list,” or I’ll pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing I can do.\n\nBut honestly, it doesn’t always stay put. One worry sort of hands off to the next one, if that makes sense, and then I look up and I’ve lost half the afternoon thinking about it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to redirect it.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries come into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly… sometimes enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. So yes, it does seem to get worse when I’m worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever pop into your head when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that does happen. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s how it can go.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension easing when you're not worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I think it does ease some when I’m truly not worrying, yes… though honestly that doesn’t seem to happen as often as I’d like.\n\nMost days my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension happening at the exact moment you start worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice it starting like a switch flipping.\n\nWhat I do notice is… when I’m in that worrying place, my shoulders and neck are already clenched, almost all day sometimes, like I’m bracing myself. That’s honestly one of the things that pushed me to come in. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly—badly enough that playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it feels connected. I just can’t always pinpoint the exact second it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension start at the same time as your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, more or less, though I don’t always catch it right when it begins.\n\nWhat I notice is that when the worrying is going on, my shoulders and neck are just clenched—sometimes most of the day—like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw aches enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar.\n\nThat was honestly part of what made me think I should come in. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about things even when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I can just redirect myself, and sometimes I try to do that in a reasonable way—like telling myself, “Okay, make a list,” or “Just focus on the next practical step.”\n\nI’ll even try to pray, or go outside and weed the garden, something useful. But a lot of times one worry just sort of hands off to the next one, and before I realize it I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to stop it. It just doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are unwanted and are popping into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s right. I don’t sit down and decide to worry, if that makes sense. It just sort of shows up, and then I try to handle it the “right” way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.\n\nAnd sometimes that helps a little, but other times one worry just hands off to another, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but it does feel unwanted. I’m not trying to keep thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself these are normal concerns, you know, job things, money things, being careful around children and families. But my mind keeps circling.

I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension happening at the exact moment you start worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. But a lot of times one worry just sort of hands off to the next one, and before I realize it I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to stop it. It just doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are unwanted and are popping into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s right. I don’t sit down and decide to worry, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but it does feel unwanted. I’m not trying to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are interfering with your ability to enjoy daily activities or perform tasks at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid it is. I don’t want to overstate it, because I do still try to be responsible and keep up with things, but it’s gotten in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines, and when I have gotten interviews, I’ve kind of stumbled through them because I’m second-guessing everything. And I used to volunteer for story-time, but now I don’t really offer unless someone else is handling the planning.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affecting my day-to-day life, and it’s making it hard for me to get back into the classroom in any dependable way. But when it’s happening, it’s very upsetting, and I have a hard time getting free of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your physical symptoms, like the jaw pain and muscle tension, are significantly exaggerated or disproportionate to any actual physical problems you might have?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / preschool_or_kindergarten_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Muscle_Tension\nmanifestation: \"Honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is that my shoulders and neck stay clenched all day, like I'm bracing for a classroom observation that never ends, and by evening my jaw aches so badly I can hardly enjoy playing guitar.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I’m not sure I’d say “exaggerated,” exactly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m being dramatic or inventing it.

But honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is that my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw aches enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar, which is… that’s been hard.

So maybe it is out of proportion to anything physically wrong, I don’t know. It just feels very real when it’s happening. Does that answer what you mean?

I just thought maybe I should get some help with it, if that’s the right thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Does the pain in your jaw stay just in your jaw or does it spread to other areas?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the jaw, I think… but it’s kind of tied in with my neck and shoulders too. One worry sort of hands off to the next one, if that makes sense, and then I look up and I’ve lost half the afternoon thinking about it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to redirect it.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries come into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly… sometimes enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. So yes, it does seem to get worse when I’m worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever pop into your head when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that does happen. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s how it can go.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension easing when you're not worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I think it does ease some when I’m truly not worrying, yes… though honestly that doesn’t seem to happen as often as I’d like.\n\nMost days my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension happening at the exact moment you start worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice it starting like a switch flipping.\n\nWhat I do notice is… when I’m in that worrying place, my shoulders and neck are already clenched, almost all day sometimes, like I’m bracing myself. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly—badly enough that playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it feels connected. And by evening my jaw aches enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar.\n\nThat was honestly part of what made me think I should come in. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about things even when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I can just redirect myself, and sometimes I try to do that in a reasonable way—like telling myself, “Okay, make a list,” or “Just focus on the next practical step.”\n\nI’ll even try to pray, or go outside and weed the garden, something useful. But a lot of times one worry just sort of hands off to the next one, and before I realize it I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to stop it. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly… sometimes enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s how it can go.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension easing when you're not worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I think it does ease some when I’m truly not worrying, yes… though honestly that doesn’t seem to happen as often as I’d like.\n\nMost days my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension happening at the exact moment you start worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice it starting like a switch flipping.\n\nWhat I do notice is… when I’m in that worrying place, my shoulders and neck are already clenched, almost all day sometimes, like I’m bracing myself. That’s honestly one of the things that pushed me to come in. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly—badly enough that playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it feels connected. And by evening my jaw aches enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar.\n\nThat was honestly part of what made me think I should come in. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about things even when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I can just redirect myself, and sometimes I try to do that in a reasonable way—like telling myself, “Okay, make a list,” or “Just focus on the next practical step.”\n\nI’ll even try to pray, or go outside and weed the garden, something useful. But a lot of times one worry just sort of hands off to the next one, and before I realize it I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m not trying hard enough, but I really do try to stop it. It just doesn’t always listen, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries are unwanted and are popping into your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s right. I don’t sit down and decide to worry, if that makes sense. It just sort of shows up, and then I try to handle it the “right” way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.\n\nAnd sometimes that helps a little, but other times one worry just hands off to another, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but it does feel unwanted. I don’t want to overstate it, because I do still try to be responsible and keep up with things, but it’s gotten in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines, and when I have gotten interviews, I’ve kind of stumbled through them because I’m second-guessing everything. I wish I could say it’s only when I sit down and decide to worry, but it kind of sneaks in.\n\nI’ll try to handle it in a reasonable way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step—but then one concern sort of hands off to another. And before I realize it, I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds like I’m not managing it very well, but I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these worries when you don't want to be thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just sitting there indulging it or anything. I’ll tell myself, okay, make a list, or pray about it, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing.\n\nBut then one worry sort of passes it off to another one, and before I know it… I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it, but I really am trying to stop it when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these worries come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can control it for a bit, I think. I try to do the right things—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.  \n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m fully in charge of when it shows up. One worry sort of hands off to the next, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I don’t want to overstate it, but… no, I guess I don’t control it as well as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension get worse when you're worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of what finally made me come in.\n\nMy shoulders and neck just stay clenched most of the day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never actually ends. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly… sometimes enough that I can hardly enjoy playing guitar. So yes, it does seem to get worse when I’m worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries ever pop into your head when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… that does happen. I’ll be trying to think about something else, or just get through what I’m doing, and then a worry kind of slips in.\n\nI try to handle it sensibly—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical step. But then one thing leads to another, and it’s like the worry hands itself off to the next one. I’ll look up and realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit much, but that’s how it can go.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension easing when you're not worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I think it does ease some when I’m truly not worrying, yes… though honestly that doesn’t seem to happen as often as I’d like.\n\nMost days my shoulders and neck feel clenched almost all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. And by evening my jaw can ache enough that it gets hard to enjoy playing guitar. I don’t know if I’m explaining that the right way, but it definitely seems tied together.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your jaw pain and muscle tension happening at the exact moment you start worrying about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s always the exact moment, if that makes sense. I don’t usually notice it starting like a switch flipping.\n\nWhat I do notice is… when I’m in that worrying place, my shoulders and neck are already clenched, almost all day sometimes, like I’m bracing myself. And by evening my jaw can ache pretty badly—badly enough that playing guitar isn’t really enjoyable.\n\nSo I don’t want to overstate it, but yes, it feels connected. I just can’t always pinpoint the exact second it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw pain and muscle tension start at the same time as your worrying thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, more or less, though I don’t always catch it right when it begins.\n\nWhat I notice is that when the worrying is going on, my shoulders and neck are just clenched—sometimes most of the day—like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. I don’t sit down and decide to worry, if that makes sense. It just sort of shows up, and then I try to handle it the “right” way—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step.\n\nAnd sometimes that helps a little, but other times one worry just hands off to another, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but it does feel unwanted. I’m not trying to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are interfering with your ability to enjoy daily activities or perform tasks at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid it is. I don’t want to overstate it, because I do still try to be responsible and keep up with things, but it’s gotten in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines, and when I have gotten interviews, I’ve kind of stumbled through them because I’m second-guessing everything. And I used to volunteer for story-time, but now I don’t really offer unless someone else is handling the planning.\n\nSo… yes, it’s affecting my day-to-day life, and it’s making it hard for me to get back into the classroom in any dependable way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself these are normal concerns, you know, job things, money things, being careful around children and families. But my mind keeps circling.\n\nIt goes from whether I’ll ever get hired again, to whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, to whether retirement is slipping away… and then sometimes it gets almost embarrassing, like if I made a small mistake during a story-time activity, I start thinking maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.\n\nI know that probably sounds extreme. I’m not saying I believe that in a calm moment. But when it’s happening, it’s very upsetting, and I have a hard time getting free of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your physical symptoms, like the jaw pain and muscle tension, are significantly exaggerated or disproportionate to any actual physical problems you might have?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say “exaggerated,” exactly. It just feels very real when it’s happening.
